Computable Bounds for Strong Approximations with Applications
Haoyu Ye, Morgane Austern

Abstract
The Komlós–Major–Tusnády (KMT) inequality for partial sums is one of the most celebrated results in probability theory. Yet its practical application is hindered by its dependence on unknown constants. This paper addresses this limitation for bounded i.i.d. random variables. At the cost of an additional logarithmic factor, we propose a computable version of the KMT inequality that depends only on the variables’ range and standard deviation. We also derive an empirical version of the inequality that achieves nominal coverage even when the standard deviation is unknown. We then demonstrate the practicality of our bounds through applications to online change point detection and first hitting time probabilities.



1
Introduction

Strong approximations—couplings between the partial sums of independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) random variables and a Brownian motion—are a cornerstone of modern probability and statistics. The celebrated Komlós–Major–Tusnády (KMT) inequality
komlos1975approximation
;
komlos1976approximation
provides the canonical result in this area, demonstrating that the coupling can be achieved with a maximal deviation that grows only logarithmically with the sample size. Specifically, the KMT inequality states that if
(Y_{i})
is a sequence of i.i.d. random variables satisfying
\inf_{a>0}\mathbb{E}[e^{a|Y_{1}|}]<\infty
, then there exist positive constants
K_{1},K_{2}<\infty
and a sequence of i.i.d. Gaussian random variables
(G_{i})\overset{i.i.d}{\sim}\mathcal{N}\big(\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}],\rm{Var}(Y_{1})\big)
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
:

\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i\leq n}\big|\sum_{j\leq i}Y_{j}-\sum_{j\leq i}G_{j}\big|\geq K_{1}\log n-K_{2}\log\alpha\Big)\leq\alpha.

This landmark result has spurred decades of theoretical development and found wide-ranging applications across probability and statistics. The original framework has been substantially extended to handle more complex settings, including multivariate processes, dependent random variables, and weighted approximations (e.g.,
sakhanenko1989accuracy
;
zaitsev2003estimates
;
berkes2014komlos
;
mies2023sequential
). We refer the reader to
mason2012quantile
for a comprehensive survey. In parallel, the KMT inequality has become a powerful tool for establishing asymptotic theory in diverse fields such as the asymptotic equivalence of statistical experiments
nussbaum1996asymptotic
, change-point detection
frick2014multiscale
;
chen2022inference
, queueing theory
nussbaum1996asymptotic
, first-passage times for Markov Chains (e.g.,
portnoy1978probability
;
lai1979first
), finance
kifer2013dynkin
;
lamberton2000optimal
;
walsh2003rate
and sequential inference (e.g.,
waudby2023distribution
).

However, a significant practical limitation of the KMT inequality is the lack of computable constants: the constants
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are not explicitly known and depend on the distribution of
(Y_{i})
in a complex way. Consequently, while the KMT inequality is a powerful tool for studying the asymptotics of statistical methods, it does not provide finite-sample statistical guarantees.
Obtaining such guarantees requires a
computable
bound on the maximum deviation between
\sum_{j\leq i}Y_{j}
and
\sum_{j\leq i}G_{j}
. In other words, the constants
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
need to be known. Very recently,
waudby2025nonasymptotic
established that these constants can be uniformly upper-bounded for all distributions sharing a uniform lower bound on their Sakhanenko parameter. Note, however, they remain uncomputable, as the bounds provided in
waudby2025nonasymptotic
rely on some unknown universal constants.
In some specific cases, the constants can be inferred. A notable approach is the Stein-based method for strong embeddings proposed in
chatterjee2012new
; their result, however, relies on the existence of a bounded Stein kernel, which is an unrealistic assumption. By adapting this method,
Bhattacharjee16
extended the approach to random variables that take values in a finite alphabet and have a null third moment. However, the bounds proposed in
Bhattacharjee16
grow quadratically with the size of the alphabet, and the assumption that the support of
(Y_{i})
is finite is itself restrictive.

In this paper, we derive
computable
thresholds
(\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha))_{k\leq n}
for the KMT inequality under the assumption that the i.i.d. random variables
(Y_{i})
are bounded, i.e.,
R:=\|Y_{1}\|_{\infty}<\infty
. For any confidence level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, these thresholds (see
algorithm

1
and
theorem

2.4
) satisfy

\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists i\leq n\penalty\ \textrm{s.t.}\penalty\ \big|\sum_{j\leq i}Y_{j}-\sum_{j\leq i}G_{j}\big|>\mathcal{D}_{i}(\alpha)\Big)\leq\alpha.

Crucially, these bounds can be computed using only the range
R
and the standard deviation
\sigma:=\sqrt{\rm{Var}(Y_{1})}
, quantities often readily available.
The price of this computability, however, is a slightly suboptimal rate of convergence: the thresholds grow as
\max_{k\leq n}\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha)=O\big(\log n(\log n-\log\alpha)\big)
, which includes an additional logarithmic factor compared to the original KMT inequality. Nevertheless, this constitutes the first computable KMT inequality that holds for all bounded random variables, without any further assumption. Even in the specific case where
(Y_{i})
take values in a finite alphabet, we demonstrate (see
appendix

J
) that our thresholds are significantly tighter, for any practical sample sizes, than those proposed in
Bhattacharjee16
.
Finally, since the standard deviation
\sigma
is often unknown in practice, we also provide in
section

3
an empirical version of our KMT inequality that does not require prior knowledge of
\sigma
. Our proof rests on two key components: a novel inductive construction and a computable conditional Wasserstein-
p
bound.
See
section

6
for more details.

We demonstrate the utility of our bounds in two distinct settings. First, we use them to derive thresholds for the CUSUM statistic in online change point detection. The proposed thresholds guarantee control of the Type I error—that is, the probability of falsely detecting a change point when none exists—uniformly over time. Our method achieves an improved detection performance compared to existing approaches. Second, we provide non-asymptotic bounds on first-hitting-time probabilities for random walks. We apply our result to the setting considered in
busani2020bounds
, who developed a novel proof technique specifically because the incomputable constants of the KMT inequality hindered a direct analysis (see page 2 of
busani2020bounds
). Our method yields a similar convergence rate but with significantly smaller constants.
In both applications, our bounds are non-asymptotic and fully computable.

1.1
Outline of the paper

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
Section

2
presents our main results together with the algorithm and theoretical guarantees.
Section

3
provides our empirical bounds.
Section

4
demonstrates two applications. In
section

5
, we show our computable upper bound for the conditional Wasserstein-
p
distance. Finally,
section

6
discusses the proof technique and the limitations of our results.

2
Main results

2.1
Assumptions and notations

We first state the assumptions under which we derive our results.

Assumption

(Assumption
(R,\sigma)
)
.

The random variables
(Y_{i})_{i\geq 1}
are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.), with
\mathrm{Var}(Y_{1})=\sigma^{2}
and
Y_{1}\in[0,R]
almost surely. Define
X_{i}:=Y_{i}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}]
as the centered version of
Y_{i}
.

Throughout the paper, we denote
\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})
the unordered multiset
\{X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\}
and write

S_{n}:=\sum_{i\leq n}X_{i}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad W_{k}:=S_{k}-\frac{k}{n}S_{n}=S_{k}-\mathbb{E}[S_{k}|S_{n}].

We adopt the standard convention that
S_{0}=0
and
W_{0}=0
. Let
\Sigma_{n}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
denote the covariance matrix of
(S_{k})_{k\leq n}
, i.e.,

\Sigma_{n,i,j}=\sigma^{2}\min\{i,j\},\qquad\forall i,j\leq n.

Additionally,
(U_{i})_{i\geq 1}
denotes a sequence of i.i.d.
\textnormal{Uniform}[0,1]
random variables independent of
(Y_{i})
. These are used to construct couplings between potentially discrete random variables and continuous ones.

We will use the Wasserstein-
p
distance to measure the discrepancy between two probability distributions.

Definition 1

(Wasserstein Distance)
.

For two probability distributions
\mu
and
\nu
on the real line, the Wasserstein-
p
distance is defined as

\mathcal{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)}\int|x-y|^{p}\,d\gamma(x,y)}}\right)^{1/p},

where
\Gamma(\mu,\nu)
is the set of all couplings with marginals
\mu
and
\nu
.

By abuse of notation, we sometimes write
\mathcal{W}_{p}(Z,Z^{\prime})
to denote the distance between the distributions of
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
, or
\mathcal{W}_{p}(Z,\nu)
to denote the distance between the distribution of
Z
and
\nu
.

We will also use the following conditional Wasserstein-
p
distance.

Definition 2

(Conditional Wasserstein Distance)
.

Let
Z,Z^{\prime}
be two random variables on the same probability space as
(X_{i})
.
We define the conditional Wasserstein-
p
distance as

\mathcal{W}_{p}(Z,Z^{\prime}|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(Z,Z^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))}\int|x-y|^{p}\,d\gamma(x,y)}}\right)^{1/p},

where
\Gamma(Z,Z^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))
is the set of all couplings whose marginals are the conditional distributions of
Z,Z^{\prime}
given
\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})
.

Similarly, by abuse of notation, we will sometimes write

\mathcal{W}_{p}(Z,\nu|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))
to denote the distance between the conditional distribution of
Z
knowing
\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})
and
\nu
.

2.2
Inductive step and proof overview

In this subsection, we outline an inductive approach to constructing a Gaussian process coupling for
(S_{k})_{1\leq k\leq n}
. More details can be found in
appendices

K
,
C
and
B
.

Our goal is to approximate the sum
(S_{k})_{k\leq n}
with a carefully constructed process
(Z_{k})_{k\leq n}
, where
Z_{k}:=\sum_{i\leq k}G_{i}
, with
(G_{i})_{i\geq 1}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
. Specifically, given
\alpha>0
, we aim to construct
(Z_{k})_{k\leq n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{n})
such that there exists a sequence
(\delta_{k})_{k\leq n}
satisfying
\max_{k\leq n}\delta_{k}=O((\log n)^{2})
and

\mathbb{P}\big(\exists k\leq n\text{ s.t. }|S_{k}-Z_{k}|\geq\delta_{k}\big)\leq\alpha.

To achieve this, we construct
(Z_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
inductively, starting from
Z_{n}
. This is done by exploiting
lemma

B.3
, which allows one to translate a Wasserstein distance bound into a probability bound. Specifically, according to
lemma

K.9
, we have
\mathcal{W}_{p}(S_{n},\mathcal{N}(0,n\sigma^{2}))\leq s^{R}_{p}(n,\sigma)
, where
s^{R}_{p}(n,\sigma)
is a computable quantity defined in
lemma

K.9
.
By
lemma

B.3
, it follows that for any
\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha
, there exists
Z_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,n\sigma^{2})
such that, defining

\delta^{\star}:=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathbb{N},\\
p\geq 2\end{subarray}}\frac{s^{R}_{p}(n,\sigma)}{\alpha_{1}^{1/p}},

we have

\mathbb{P}\big(|Z_{n}-S_{n}|\geq\delta^{\star}\big)\leq\alpha_{0}.

Interestingly,
Z_{n}
can be chosen to be measurable with respect to
\sigma(S_{n},U_{n})
, where
U_{n}
is an independent
\textnormal{Uniform}[0,1]
random variable (see
lemma

B.2
).

Once we have chosen
Z_{n}
, it remains to build
(Z_{k})_{k\leq n}
in a way that ensures it has the correct joint distribution and that the difference
|Z_{k}-S_{k}|
is small. As
Z_{n}\in\sigma(U_{n},S_{n})
, if
(\tilde{Z}_{k})
are normally distributed conditionally on
S_{n}
and
U_{n}
, meaning that
(\tilde{Z}_{k})\big|S_{n},U_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tilde{\Sigma}_{n})
where
\tilde{\Sigma}_{n,i,j}=\sigma^{2}(\min\{i,j\}-\frac{ij}{n})
, then the process
\big(\tilde{Z}_{j}+\frac{j}{n}Z_{n}\big)_{j\leq n}
is a centered Gaussian vector with covariance
\Sigma_{n}
and is hence a candidate for
(Z_{k})_{k\leq n}
. The next step therefore consists of carefully building
(\tilde{Z}_{k})_{k\leq n}
to satisfy:

Assumption 1

(Conditionally coupled on
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathbb{N}
)
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
with
a\leq b
, and define
\mathcal{I}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\llbracket a,b}}\right\rrbracket
.
Let
(\tilde{Z}_{k})_{k\in\mathcal{I}}
be a sequence of random variables. We say that
(\tilde{Z}_{k})_{k\in\mathcal{I}}
is conditionally coupled with
(X_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
on
\mathcal{I}
if it is measurable with respect to
\sigma((X_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}},(U_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}})
and its conditional marginal distribution satisfies

(\tilde{Z}_{k})_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\Big|\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}X_{i},U_{{b}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Var}(W_{\mathcal{I}})),

where
W_{\mathcal{I}}
designates the vector with
k
-th coordinate
W_{\mathcal{I},k}=\sum_{\ell\leq k,\ell\in\mathcal{I}}X_{\ell}-\frac{|\llbracket k\rrbracket\cap\mathcal{I}|}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{I}}X_{\ell}
, for
k\in\mathcal{I}
.

In the next lemma, we show how
(\tilde{Z}_{k})_{k\leq n}
and
(\delta_{k})_{k\leq n}
can be built recursively.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
(Y_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be generated according to Assumption (
R,\sigma
). Let
(\delta_{k})_{k\leq n}
be a sequence of positive reals satisfying
\delta_{k}=\delta_{n-k}
for all
k>n/2
. Suppose that
n
is even and that one can construct
(\tilde{Z}_{k}^{1})
satisfying
1
with
\mathcal{I}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\llbracket 1,\frac{n}{2}}}\right\rrbracket
. For all
k\leq n/2
, denote
W_{k}^{1}:=S_{k}-\frac{2k}{n}S_{n/2}
. Then there exists
(\tilde{Z}_{k})
satisfying
1
with
\mathcal{I}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\llbracket 1,n}}\right\rrbracket
such that the following holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\exists k\leq n\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ |W_{k}-\tilde{Z}_{k}|\geq\delta_{k}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}\big(\exists k\leq n/2\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ |W^{1}_{k}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{2k}{n}\delta_{n/2}\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(\exists k\leq n/2\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ |W^{1}_{k}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{2k}{n}\delta_{n/2}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle+\inf_{p\geq 2}\Big(\frac{\omega_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma)}{\delta_{n/2}}\Big)^{p},

where
\omega_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma)
is defined in
theorem

5.2

Proof.

Firstly, we denote

\tilde{W}^{1}_{k}=S_{k}-\frac{2k}{n}S_{n/2}\quad\text{for }k=1,\dots,n/2,

and

\tilde{W}^{2}_{k}=S_{k}-S_{n/2}-\frac{2(k-n/2)}{n}(S_{n}-S_{n/2})\quad\text{for }k=n/2,\dots,n.

Let
(\tilde{Z}^{1}_{i})_{i\leq n/2}
be a Gaussian vector satisfying
1
with
\mathcal{I}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\llbracket 1,\frac{n}{2}}}\right\rrbracket
. Note that as
(X_{i})_{i>\frac{n}{2}}
have the same distribution as
(X_{i})_{i\leq\frac{n}{2}}
, we have that there also exists
(\tilde{Z}^{2}_{i})_{i>\frac{n}{2}}
that satisfy
1
with
\mathcal{I}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\llbracket n/2+1,n}}\right\rrbracket
and is such that

\displaystyle\Big((\tilde{W}_{k}^{1})_{k\leq n/2},(\tilde{Z}_{k}^{1})_{k\leq n/2}\Big)\overset{d}{=}-\Big((\tilde{W}_{n-k}^{2})_{k\leq n/2},(\tilde{Z}_{n-k}^{2})_{k<n/2}\Big).

We remark that conditionally on
\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})
,
W_{n/2}
is a discrete random variable. Let
F_{n/2}(\cdot|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))
designates its conditional CDF and
p_{n/2}(\cdot|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))
its conditional p.m.f. We define

\tilde{F}^{U_{n/2}}_{n/2}(x)=F_{n/2}(x^{-}|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))+U_{n/2}\cdot p_{n/2}(x|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})).

(1)

and set

\tilde{Z}_{n/2}:=\frac{\sqrt{n}\sigma}{2}\Phi^{-1}(\tilde{F}^{U_{n/2}}_{n/2}(W_{n/2})).

Using
lemma

B.2
, we know that

\tilde{Z}_{n/2}|U_{n},S_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{4}).

We will build
(\tilde{Z}_{k})
by linear interpolation between
(\tilde{Z}_{k}^{1}),(\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2})
, and
\tilde{Z}_{n/2}
. More precisely, we define

\tilde{Z}_{k}:=\begin{cases}\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}+\dfrac{2k}{n}\tilde{Z}_{n/2}\quad&\text{if }k<n/2,\\
\tilde{Z}_{n/2}\quad&\text{if }k=n/2,\\
\tilde{Z}^{2}_{k}+\dfrac{2n-2k}{n}\tilde{Z}_{n/2}\quad&\text{if }k>n/2.\end{cases}

(2)

We first remark that, since
(\tilde{Z}_{k}^{1})
and
(\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2})
are chosen to satisfy
1
on
\mathcal{I}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\llbracket 1,\frac{n}{2}}}\right\rrbracket
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\llbracket n/2+1,n}}\right\rrbracket
respectively, we obtain that, conditionally on
U_{n},U_{n/2},S_{n/2},\text{ and }S_{n}-S_{n/2}
, the random vector
(\tilde{Z}_{k})
is Gaussian with mean
\Big(\frac{2}{n}\tilde{Z}_{n/2},\dots,\tilde{Z}_{n/2},\dots,\frac{2}{n}\tilde{Z}_{n/2}\Big)^{\top}
and covariance

\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Var}(W_{1:\frac{n}{2}})&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathrm{Var}(W_{1:n/2})\end{bmatrix}.

Hence, using the fact that Gaussian distributions are conjugate priors, we obtain that
(\tilde{Z}_{k})
satisfies
1
for
\mathcal{I}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\llbracket 1,n}}\right\rrbracket
. Hence, to prove
lemma

2.1
, it remains to bound
|\tilde{Z}_{k}-W_{k}|
carefully. In this goal, we note, by a union bound argument, that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\exists k\leq n\textrm{ s.t. }|W_{k}-\tilde{Z}_{k}|\geq\delta_{k})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}|\geq\delta_{n/2}\Big)+\mathbb{P}\Big(|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}|<\delta_{n/2}\textrm{ and }\exists k\leq n\penalty\ |W_{k}-\tilde{Z}_{k}|\geq\delta_{k}\Big)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}|\geq\delta_{n/2}\Big)+(D).

We bound each term successively. Firstly, using Markov’s inequality, we obtain that for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}|\geq\delta_{n/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}\|_{p}^{p}}{\delta_{n/2}^{p}}.

Moreover, according to
lemma

B.4
, we have that
\|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}\|_{p}=\Big\|\mathcal{W}_{p}\big(W_{n/2},\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{4})\big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\big)\big\|_{p}.
This directly implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}|\geq\delta_{n/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{p\geq 2}\frac{\Big\|\mathcal{W}_{p}\big(W_{n/2},\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{4})\big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\big)\Big\|_{p}^{p}}{\delta_{n/2}^{p}}.

According to
theorem

5.2
we know that for all
p\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{W}_{p}\big(W_{n/2},\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{4})\big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\big)\Big\|_{p}\leq\omega_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma).

(3)

The definition of
\omega_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma)
can be found in
theorem

5.2
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}|\geq\delta_{n/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{p\geq 2}\Big\{\frac{\omega_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma)}{\delta_{n/2}}\Big\}^{p},

Then to bound
(D)
, suppose that
|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}|<\delta_{n/2}
but that there exists
k\leq n
such that
|W_{k}-\tilde{Z}_{k}|\geq\delta_{k}.
If
k<\frac{n}{2}
, then we have

\displaystyle\delta_{k}\geq

\displaystyle\big|W_{k}-\tilde{Z}_{k}\big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}+\frac{k}{n/2}(W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\Big|+\frac{k}{n/2}\Big|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\Big|+\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2},

which implies that

\big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\big|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}.

Moreover, similarly if
k>n/2
, then since

W_{k}-\tilde{Z}_{k}-\frac{n-k}{n/2}(W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2})=W_{k-n/2}^{2}-\tilde{Z}^{2}_{k-n/2},

we have

\big|W_{k}^{2}-\tilde{Z}^{2}_{k}\big|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{n-k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}.

Hence this implies that

\displaystyle(D)\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k<n/2,|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}_{k}^{1}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\textrm{ or }\penalty\ \exists k>n/2,|W_{k}^{2}-\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{n-k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big).

We remark that
(\tilde{Z}_{k}^{1})
is, by assumption, measurable with respect to
X_{1:n/2}
and
U_{1:n/2}
. Similarly, by assumption, we have
(\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2})
is measurable with respect to
X_{n/2+1:n}
and
U_{n/2+1:n}
.
Hence, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k<n/2\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ \big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\big|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\penalty\ \textrm{or}\penalty\ \exists k>n/2\textrm{\penalty\ s.t\penalty\ }|W_{k}^{2}-\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{n-k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{I}\Big(\exists k<n/2\penalty\ \textrm{s.t,}\penalty\ \big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\big|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k>n/2\textrm{\penalty\ s.t.\penalty\ }|W_{k}^{2}-\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{n-k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big|X_{1:n/2},U_{1:n/2}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{I}\Big(\exists k<n/2\penalty\ \textrm{s.t,}\penalty\ \big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\big|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big)\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k>n/2\textrm{\penalty\ s.t.\penalty\ }|W_{k}^{2}-\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{n-k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\overset{}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k<n/2\penalty\ \textrm{s.t.}\penalty\ \big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\big|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big)\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k>n/2\textrm{\penalty\ s.t.\penalty\ }|W_{k}^{2}-\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{n-k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big),

where (a) comes from the tower law, (b) from the fact that
(W_{k}^{2},\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2})
are independent of
X_{1:n/2},U_{1:n/2}
.
Hence, as for any events
A,B
, we have
\mathbb{P}(A\cup B)=\mathbb{P}(A)+\mathbb{P}(B)-\mathbb{P}(A\cap B)
we obtain that

\displaystyle(D)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k<n/2\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ \big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\big|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big)+\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k>n/2\textrm{\penalty\ s.t\penalty\ }|W_{k}^{2}-\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{n-k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k<n/2\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ \big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\big|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big)\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k>n/2\textrm{\penalty\ s.t\penalty\ }|W_{k}^{2}-\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{n-k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big).

Finally we remark that

\displaystyle\Big((\tilde{W}_{k}^{1})_{k\leq n/2},(\tilde{Z}_{k}^{1})_{k\leq n/2}\Big)\overset{d}{=}-\Big((\tilde{W}_{n-k}^{2})_{k\leq n/2},(\tilde{Z}_{n-k}^{2})_{k<n/2}\Big).

Hence, as
\delta_{n-k}=\delta_{k}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k>n/2\textrm{\penalty\ s.t\penalty\ }|W_{k}^{2}-\tilde{Z}_{k}^{2}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{n-k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k<n/2\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ \big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\big|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big).

This directly implies that

\displaystyle(D)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k<n/2\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ \big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\big|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k<n/2\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ \big|W_{k}^{1}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}\big|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\Big)^{2}.

∎

Lemma
2.1
tells us that if we choose

\delta_{n/2}\geq\inf_{p\geq 2}\alpha_{0}^{1/p}\omega_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma),

then, as long as we can construct
(\tilde{Z}_{k}^{1})
to satisfy
1
with
\mathcal{I}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\llbracket 1,n/2}}\right\rrbracket
, there exists
(\tilde{Z}_{k})
that satisfies
1
with
\mathcal{I}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\llbracket 1,n}}\right\rrbracket
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\exists k\leq n\textrm{ s.t. }|W_{k}-\tilde{Z}_{k}|\geq\delta_{k}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}\big(\exists k\leq n/2\textrm{ s.t. }|W^{1}_{k}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\big)

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbb{P}\big(\exists k\leq n/2\textrm{ s.t. }|W^{1}_{k}-\tilde{Z}^{1}_{k}|\geq\delta_{k}-\frac{k}{n/2}\delta_{n/2}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha_{0}.

Hence, it remains to choose
(\delta_{k})_{k<n/2}
for all
k<n/2
. However, we note that this step involves only half of the data, namely
X_{1},\dots,X_{n/2}
, suggesting that constructing
(\delta_{k})
can be done inductively.

2.3
Algorithm and theoretical guarantees

In this subsection, we exploit
lemma

2.1
to introduce our main algorithm, which allows us to couple both
(W_{k})
and
(S_{k})
with Gaussian vectors. In this goal, for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote

W_{k}^{\ell}=S_{k}-\frac{k}{2^{\ell}}S_{2^{\ell}}\qquad\forall k\leq 2^{\ell}.

Moreover, we set
\delta_{1}^{0}:=0
. For all
\ell\geq 1
and
\alpha>0
, define
\delta_{2^{\ell-1}}^{\ell}
as:

\delta_{2^{\ell-1}}^{\ell}(\alpha):=\inf_{p\geq 2}\alpha^{1/p}\omega_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma),

where
\omega_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma)
is the computable upper bound introduced in
eq.

3
and defined in
theorem

5.2
. For all other
k\leq 2^{\ell}
, define
\delta_{k}^{\ell}(\alpha)
recursively as follows:

\displaystyle\delta_{k}^{\ell}(\alpha):=\begin{cases}\delta_{k}^{\ell-1}(\alpha)+\frac{k}{2^{\ell-1}}\delta_{2^{\ell-1}}^{\ell}(\alpha),&\text{if }k\leq 2^{\ell-1},\\
\delta_{2^{\ell}-k}^{\ell-1}(\alpha)+\frac{2^{\ell}-k}{2^{\ell-1}}\delta_{2^{\ell-1}}^{\ell}(\alpha),&\text{if }k>2^{\ell-1}.\end{cases}

(4)

With this definition, we can establish the following result.

Theorem 2.2
.

Suppose that
(Y_{i})_{i\geq 1}
satisfies Assumption (
R,\sigma
). Let
\alpha_{0}>0
be a constant and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
. Let
(\delta_{k})
be defined as
\delta_{k}:=\delta_{k}^{L}(\alpha_{0})
where
L=\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil
. Then there exists
(\tilde{Z}_{k})
satisfying
1
with
\mathcal{I}=\llbracket 1,2^{L}\rrbracket
such that the following holds

\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k\leq n\penalty\ \textrm{s.t.}\penalty\ |W_{k}-\tilde{Z}_{k}|>\delta_{k}\Big)\leq\beta_{L},

where
\beta_{L}
is defined recursively by
\beta_{0}:=0
and
\beta_{\ell}:=2\beta_{\ell-1}-\beta_{\ell-1}^{2}+\alpha_{0}
for all
\ell\geq 1
.

The proof can be found in
appendix

D
.
This can be further used to couple
(S_{k})
with a Gaussian vector and obtain the following result:

Corollary 2.2.1
.

Suppose that
theorem

2.2
holds with some
\alpha_{0}>0
and
n=2^{L}
for
L\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\alpha_{1}>0
be a constant, and define
\delta^{\star}(\alpha_{1}):=\inf_{p\geq 2}\alpha_{1}^{1/p}s_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma)
, where
s_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma)
is defined in
lemma

K.9
. Then there exists a centered Gaussian vector
(Z_{k})_{1\leq k\leq n}
with the same covariance structure as
(S_{k})_{1\leq k\leq n}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k\leq n\penalty\ \textrm{s.t.}\penalty\ |S_{k}-Z_{k}|\geq\delta_{k}+\frac{k}{n}\delta^{*}(\alpha_{1})\Big)\leq\beta_{L}+\alpha_{1},

(5)

where
\delta_{k}:=\delta_{k}^{L}(\alpha_{0})
and
\beta_{L}
is defined recursively by
\beta_{0}:=0
and
\beta_{\ell}:=2\beta_{\ell-1}-\beta_{\ell-1}^{2}+\alpha_{0}
for all
\ell\geq 1
.

The proof can be found in
appendix

D
. We note that, for a fixed
\alpha_{0}
,
\beta_{L}
increases toward
1
as
L=\log_{2}(n)
increases. Therefore, to guarantee a given coverage level,
\alpha_{0}
must depend on the sample size
n
. For a given
n
, we choose
\alpha_{0}
algorithmically to obtain the desired coverage level. This is implemented in
algorithm

1
for a specified coverage
\alpha>0
.

Input:

An integer
n>0
,
R>0
,
\sigma>0
, total probability budget
\alpha>0

Output:

Thresholds
(\Delta_{k}(\alpha))_{k\leq n}

Define:

L:=\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil

//
Step 1: Determine
\nu_{0}
based on the chosen
\alpha

Initialize for
\nu_{0}
search:
Choose an initial guess for
\nu_{0}
and set
\beta_{0}(\nu_{0})=0

Compute
\beta_{k}
recursively:

for

k=1

to

L

do

\beta_{k}(\nu_{0})\leftarrow 2\beta_{k-1}(\nu_{0})-\beta_{k-1}^{2}(\nu_{0})+\nu_{0}

Finalize
\nu_{0}
:
Find the largest
\nu_{0}^{*}
such that
\beta_{L}(\nu_{0}^{*})\leq\alpha

//
Step 2: Calculate intermediate
\delta^{M}_{k}
values using
\nu_{0}^{*}

\delta_{1}^{0}\leftarrow 0

for

M=1

to

L

do

\delta_{2^{M-1}}^{M}\leftarrow\min_{2\leq p}\frac{\omega_{p}^{R}(2^{M},\sigma)}{(\nu_{0}^{*})^{1/p}}

for

k=1

to

2^{M-1}

do

\delta_{k}^{M}\leftarrow\delta_{k}^{M-1}+\frac{k}{2^{M-1}}\delta_{2^{M-1}}^{M}

for

k=2^{M-1}+1

to

2^{M}-1

do

\delta_{k}^{M}\leftarrow\delta_{2^{M}-k}^{M-1}+\frac{2^{M}-k}{2^{M-1}}\delta_{2^{M-1}}^{M}

Let
\delta^{L}_{k}
denote the final values
\delta_{k}^{L}
from this step.

for

k=1

to

n

do

\Delta_{k}(\alpha)\leftarrow\delta^{L}_{k}

return

\Delta_{k}(\alpha)

Algorithm 1

Computing
(\Delta_{k}(\alpha))_{k\leq n}

Input:

An integer
n>0
,
R>0
,
\sigma>0
, total probability budget
\alpha>0

Output:

Thresholds
(\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha))
minimized by optimal choice of
\alpha_{0}

Define:

L:=\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil

//
Step 1: For any candidate split
\alpha_{0}\in(0,\alpha)
, compute thresholds

Given
\alpha_{0}\in(0,\alpha)
and
\alpha_{1}:=\alpha-\alpha_{0}
:

//
Step 1a: Compute
\Delta_{k}
using
\alpha_{0}

for

k=1

to

n

do

\Delta_{k}(\alpha_{0})\leftarrow\text{output of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ts122} with input }n\text{ and }\alpha_{0}

//
Step 1b: Compute
\delta^{\star}
using
\alpha_{1}

\delta^{\star}(\alpha_{1})\leftarrow\min_{2\leq p}\frac{s_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma)}{(\alpha_{1})^{1/p}}

//
Step 1c: Compute
\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha)
, values to be minimized

for

to

n

\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha)\leftarrow\Delta_{k}(\alpha_{0})+\frac{k}{n}\delta^{\star}(\alpha_{1})

Find

. Let
\alpha_{1}^{*}:=\alpha-\alpha_{0}^{*}

for

k=1

to

n

\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha)\leftarrow\Delta_{k}(\alpha^{*}_{0})+\frac{k}{n}\delta^{\star}(\alpha_{1}^{*})

//
Step 3: Return optimized thresholds

return

(\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha))_{k\leq n}

Algorithm 2

Computing
(\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha))_{k\leq n}

Theorem 2.3
.

Suppose that
(Y_{i})_{i\geq 1}
satisfy Assumption (
R,\sigma
).
Let
\alpha>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
. Define
(\Delta_{k}(\alpha))
as the output of
algorithm

1
.
Then there exists a centered Gaussian vector
\tilde{Z}:=(\tilde{Z}_{k})_{0\leq k\leq n}
with the same covariance structure as
(W_{k})_{0\leq k\leq n}
such that the following inequality holds:

\mathbb{P}(\exists k\leq n\textrm{ s.t. }\big|W_{k}-\tilde{Z}_{k}\big|\geq\Delta_{k}(\alpha))\leq\alpha.

The proof can be found in
appendix

D
. Similarly, this leads to the following coupling lemma between
(S_{k})
and a Gaussian random vector.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose that
(Y_{i})_{i\geq 1}
satisfy Assumption (
R,\sigma
).
Let
\alpha>0
and
L\in\mathbb{N}
. Set
n=2^{L}
. Define
(\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha))
as the output of
algorithm

2
.
Then there exists a centered Gaussian vector
(Z_{k})_{k\leq n}
with covariance
\Sigma_{n}
such that the following inequality holds:

\mathbb{P}(\exists k\leq n\textrm{ s.t. }\big|S_{k}-{Z}_{k}\big|\geq\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha))\leq\alpha.

The proof can be found in
appendix

D
.

Remark 2.5
.

In practice, we use a slightly modified version of
algorithm

2
and
algorithm

1
that, for very small
\ell
, allows the use of alternative bounds other than the conditional Wasserstein-
p
bound to control
\delta_{2^{\ell-1}}^{\ell}(\alpha)
. In addition, we allow the confidence level to vary with
\ell
instead of using a fixed
\alpha_{0}
. These changes do not affect the theoretical guarantees, but can offer slight improvements in practice. The corresponding algorithms details are presented in
section

H.2
.

2.4
Analysis of the rate

The size of the thresholds
(\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha))_{k\leq n}
proposed in
theorem

2.4
depends on the sample size
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
. In this subsection, we show how fast they grow with
n
and compare it to the rate given by the classical KMT inequality.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
. Let
L:=\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil
, and let
\big(\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha))_{k\leq n}
be defined as the output of
algorithm

2
. Then there exists a constant
\kappa_{R}^{\sigma}
, depending only on
R
and
\sigma
, such that the following inequality holds:

\max_{\ell\leq n}\mathcal{D}_{\ell}(\alpha)\leq L\kappa_{R}^{\sigma}(L-\log\alpha).

Moreover, for all
0<\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}\leq\frac{R}{2}
, the following holds:

\sup_{\sigma\in[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]}\kappa_{R}^{\sigma}<\infty.

The proof can be found in
lemma

C.16
.
The KMT inequality
komlos1975approximation
;
komlos1976approximation
tells us that there exist constants
K_{1},K_{2}<\infty
, depending on the distribution of
(Y_{i})
, such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
and
\alpha>0
, there exists a Gaussian vector
(Z_{k})
satisfying

\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{k\leq n}|S_{k}-Z_{k}|\geq K_{1}\log n-K_{2}\log\alpha\Big)\leq\alpha.

We note that both
\max_{k\leq n}\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha)
, where
(\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha))_{k\leq n}
is the output from
algorithm

2
, and
K_{1}\log n-K_{2}\log\alpha
grow linearly in
-\log\alpha
.
However,
\max_{\ell\leq n}\mathcal{D}_{\ell}(\alpha)
grows quadratically, rather than linearly, in
\log n
, which is a suboptimal rate. On the other hand, our thresholds are fully computable, in contrast to almost all previous bounds, and depend only on
R
and
\sigma
. We also note that, while
Bhattacharjee16
provides a computable bound, it requires that the random variables
(Y_{i})
take values from a finite alphabet and satisfy
\mathbb{E}[(Y_{i}-\mathbb{E}(Y_{1}))^{3}]=0
. In addition, their bound grows quadratically with the alphabet size. In
appendix

J
, we compare our bounds to theirs and remark that even for small alphabet sizes, our bounds are significantly tighter for any reasonable sample size. See
section

6
for comments on how this rate could be improved if the random variables
(Y_{i})
admit a Stein kernel.

3
Empirical bound: extension to unknown variance

In practice, the variance
\sigma
is often unknown and must be estimated from the data. This can in particular be done by using the empirical variance

\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{k}:=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i\leq k}(X_{i}-\frac{S_{k}}{k})^{2}

as an estimator of
\sigma
. As our KMT inequality relies on knowledge of
\sigma
, it is tempting to use
\hat{\sigma}_{k}
as a proxy for
\sigma
and, for example, to use
algorithm

2
with variance input
\hat{\sigma}_{k}
. However,
\hat{\sigma}_{k}
is a random estimator of the variance. Hence, to obtain a correct inequality, the uncertainty in our estimate of
\sigma
must be taken into account. This can, for example, be done using anytime-valid confidence sequences for
(\hat{\sigma}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
.

For clarity throughout this section, we make the dependence on
R
and
\sigma
explicit by writing

(\Delta_{k}(\alpha,R,\sigma))_{k\leq n}\quad\text{and}\quad(D_{k}(\alpha,R,\sigma))_{k\leq n}

for the outputs of
algorithm

2
and
algorithm

1
, respectively.

We consider estimators
\hat{\sigma}_{k}
that satisfy the following conditions.

Assumption 2

(Empirical variance with confidence level
\delta
)
.

Let
(\hat{\sigma}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a sequence of estimators of the variance
\sigma
, where each
\hat{\sigma}_{k}\in\sigma(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k})
depends only on the first
k
observations. Let
([\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{L},\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}])
be a sequence of intervals such that
0\leq\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{L}\leq\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}\leq\frac{R}{2}
, with
\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{L},\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}\in\sigma(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k})
for all
k
. Assume this sequence satisfies

\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ \sigma\not\in[\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{L},\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}]\Big)\leq\delta.

Note that if we chose
\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{2}:=\frac{c_{1}+\sum_{i\leq k-1}(Y_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i})^{2}}{k}
and
\hat{\mu}_{i}:=\frac{c_{2}+\sum_{i\leq i-1}Y_{i}}{i}
, where
c_{1},c_{2}\in[0,1]
are constants, then
martinez2025sharp
provides a way to build intervals
([\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{L},\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}])
that satisfy
2
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\alpha,\rho\in(0,1)
.
Let
(\hat{\sigma}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a sequence of estimators, and let
([\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{L},\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}])_{k\geq 1}
be a sequence of intervals satisfying
2
with confidence level
\delta=\rho\alpha
. Define
\tilde{R}_{k}:=\frac{R}{{\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{L}}}
.
Then there exist centered Gaussian vectors
(\tilde{Z}_{k})
and
(Z_{k})
with the same covariance structure as
(W_{k})
and
(S_{k})
respectively, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k\leq n\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ |W_{k}-\tilde{Z}_{k}|\geq\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}\penalty\ \Delta_{k}(\alpha(1-\rho),\tilde{R}_{k},1)\Big)\leq\alpha,

(6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists k\leq n\penalty\ \textrm{s.t}\penalty\ |S_{k}-Z_{k}|\geq\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}\penalty\ \mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha(1-\rho),\tilde{R}_{k},1)\Big)\leq\alpha.

(7)

The proof can be found in
appendix

E
.

Remark 3.2
.

For any time step
k
, the thresholds
\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}\penalty\ \Delta_{k}(\alpha(1-\rho),\tilde{R}_{k},1)
and
\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}\penalty\ \mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha(1-\rho),\tilde{R}_{k},1)
only depend on the first
k
observations
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k}.
This means they can be computed sequentially as new observations arrive.

4
Applications

In this subsection, we present applications of our bounds to online change point detection and first hitting time probabilities.

4.1
Online change point detection

Change point detection, the problem of detecting whether there is a change in the distribution of a sequence of independent random variables
(Y_{i})
, is a classical problem in statistics with many applications, notably in manufacturing
montgomery2020introduction
;
hawkins2012cumulative
;
woodall2014some
, finance
habibi2021bayesian
;
banerjee2020change
, and healthcare
yang2006adaptive
;
malladi2013online
;
STAUDACHER2005582
;
bosc2003automatic
.

We focus on detecting a shift in the mean in an online setting. Formally, we define a
(T,\theta)
change point to have occurred at an unknown time
T\geq 0
with magnitude
\theta\neq 0
if the sequence
(W_{i})
—where
W_{i}:=Y_{i}
for
i\leq T
and
W_{i}:=Y_{i}-\theta
for
i>T
—is identically distributed. As data arrives sequentially, our goal is to test the following hypotheses:

\displaystyle H_{0}:\penalty\ \text{No change point exists.}\qquad H_{1}:\text{A }(T,\theta)\text{ change point exists for some }T>1,\theta\neq 0.

One of the most classical statistics for this problem is the CUSUM statistic
page1954continuous
, defined as

\mathcal{T}_{s,t}:=\Big|\frac{1}{s}\sum_{i\leq s}Y_{i}-\frac{1}{t-s}\sum_{t\geq i>s}Y_{i}\Big|.

In an online setting,
The null hypothesis is rejected at current
time
t
if there exists some
s\leq t
such that
\mathcal{T}_{s,t}
exceeds a certain threshold
(\mathcal{C}_{s,t})
:

\text{Reject the null hypothesis if }\exists s\leq t\penalty\ \textrm{s.t.}\penalty\ \mathcal{T}_{s,t}>\mathcal{C}_{s,t}.

However, the literature generally does not specify how to select these thresholds to control the Type I error, i.e., the probability of false detection.

One approach, proposed by
maillard_sequential_2019
, uses double time-uniform concentration inequalities to construct valid thresholds. We instead use our computable KMT inequality. We assume only that the range of
(Y_{i})
is known, and do not require prior knowledge of the mean or standard deviation.

Proposition 4.1
.

Suppose that
(Y_{i})
satisfies Assumption (
R,\sigma
). Let
\delta,\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in(0,1)
be such that
\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}<\delta
. Let
\beta>1
, and let
(\hat{\sigma}_{k})
and
([\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{L},\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}])
be a sequence of estimators and confidence sets satisfying
2
with confidence level
\delta_{1}.

Let
g_{\beta}(t,s,\delta_{2})
be the output of
algorithm

3
, and define

\mathcal{C}_{s,t}:=\hat{\sigma}_{t}^{U}\sqrt{\frac{t}{s(t-s)}}\sqrt{(1+\frac{1}{t})2\log(\frac{(t-1)\sqrt{t+1}}{\delta-\delta_{1}-\delta_{2}})}+\frac{t}{s(t-s)}g_{\beta}(t,s,\delta_{2}).

Then, under the null hypothesis, we have

\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\Big(\exists 0<s<t\penalty\ \textrm{s.t.}\penalty\ |\mathcal{T}_{s,t}|\geq\mathcal{C}_{s,t}\Big)\leq\delta.

The proof can be found in
appendix

F
.

Remark 4.2
.

The thresholds
(\mathcal{C}_{s,t})_{s<t}
can be chosen to guarantee that the probability of false detection is less than any significance level
\delta
. Moreover, computing these thresholds only requires knowledge of the range
R=\|Y_{1}\|_{\infty}
.

Before presenting
algorithm

3
, we define the following notation: Given an increasing sequence
\mathbf{L}:=(L_{i})
of integers, we denote
\ell_{\mathbf{L}}(k):=\sup_{i}\{i\penalty\ \textrm{s.t.}\penalty\ k\geq\sum_{j\leq i}2^{L_{j}}\}
,
u_{\mathbf{L}}(k):=\inf_{i}\{i\penalty\ \textrm{s.t.}\penalty\ k\leq\sum_{j\leq i}2^{L_{j}}\}
, and
N_{i}=\sum_{j\leq i}2^{L_{j}}
. We are now ready to introduce the algorithm.

Input:

Integers
t>s>0
,
\sigma>0
,
R>0
,
\delta_{2}>0
,
\beta>1
, an increasing sequence
\mathbf{L}=(L_{i})
, a sequence of intervals
([\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{L},\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}])

Output:

g_{\beta}(t,s,\delta_{2})

for

i\geq 1

do

\delta_{2,i}\leftarrow\delta_{2}\frac{\beta^{-i}}{\sum_{j\geq 0}\beta^{-j}}
.

\Big((\tilde{\Delta}^{N_{i}}_{k}(\delta_{2,i}))_{k\in(N_{i-1},N_{i}]},\tilde{\delta}_{N_{i}}^{*}(\delta_{2,i})\Big)\leftarrow
output of
algorithm

4

with input
(N_{i-1},N_{i}]
,
R
,
\sigma
,
\delta_{2,i}
, and
([\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{L},\hat{\sigma}_{k}^{U}])

Shorthand
\tilde{\Delta}^{i}_{k}:=\tilde{\Delta}^{N_{i}}_{k}(\delta_{2,i})
and
\tilde{\delta}_{i}^{*}:=\tilde{\delta}_{N_{i}}^{*}(\delta_{2,i})

If
u_{\mathbf{L}}(s)=u_{\mathbf{L}}(t)
:

g_{\beta}(t,s,\delta_{2})\leftarrow\sum_{k\leq\ell_{\mathbf{L}}(s)}\tilde{\delta}_{k}^{*}\big(1-\frac{s}{t}\big)+\frac{s}{t}\tilde{\delta}^{*}_{u_{\mathbf{L}(s)}}+\tilde{\Delta}^{u_{\mathbf{L}}(s)}_{s}+\frac{s}{t}\tilde{\Delta}^{u_{\mathbf{L}}(s)}_{t}

If
u_{\mathbf{L}}(s)\neq u_{\mathbf{L}}(t)
:

\displaystyle g_{\beta}(t,s,\delta_{2})\leftarrow

\displaystyle\sum_{k\leq\ell_{\mathbf{L}}(s)}\tilde{\delta}_{k}^{*}\big(1-\frac{s}{t}\big)+\frac{s}{t}\sum_{u_{\mathbf{L}}(s)+1\leq k\leq\ell_{\mathbf{L}}(t)}\tilde{\delta}_{k}^{*}+\frac{s}{t}\frac{t-N_{\ell_{\mathbf{L}}(t)}}{N_{u_{\mathbf{L}}(t)}-N_{\ell_{\mathbf{L}}(t)}}\tilde{\delta}^{*}_{u_{\mathbf{L}}(t)}

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{s-N_{\ell_{\mathbf{L}}(s)}}{N_{u_{\mathbf{L}}(s)}-N_{\ell_{\mathbf{L}}(s)}}-\frac{s}{t}\Big)\tilde{\delta}^{*}_{u_{\mathbf{L}(s)}}+\tilde{\Delta}^{u_{\mathbf{L}}(s)}_{s}+\frac{s}{t}\tilde{\Delta}^{u_{\mathbf{L}}(t)}_{t}

Return

g_{\beta}(t,s,\delta_{2})

Algorithm 3

Computing
g_{\beta}(t,s,\delta_{2})

Proposition
4.1
shows that the Type I error of our proposed test is well controlled. To assess its power, we compare its detection performance against the method of
maillard_sequential_2019
.

Figure

1
presents an empirical comparison of detection rates under varying mean shifts. The observations
(Y_{i})
are defined as the average of
\ell
independent
\textnormal{Uniform}[0,1]
variables, with a change point at
T=2000
and a post-change mean increased by the specified shift.

Figure 1:

Detection rate comparison across mean shifts, with each
Y_{i}
an average of
\ell
independent
\textnormal{Uniform}[0,1]
random variables. A change point occurs at
T=2000
, with a post-change mean increased by the specified shift.

4.2
First hitting time probabilities

The first passage or hitting time
\tau
of a Markov chain
(W_{i})
is the first time it crosses a certain boundary. Studying the distribution of
\tau
plays an important role in many applications, such as finance
shreve2004stochastic
;
kou2002jump
;
chicheportiche2013applicationsfirstpassageideasfinance
, neuroscience
gerstein1964random
;
gerstner2014neuronal
, queuing theory
asmussen2003applied
, and reliability analysis
trivedi2001probability
;
howard2012dynamic
;
rausand2003system
.

In the asymptotic regime, the KMT inequality has been a key tool, allowing one to reduce the study of
\tau
to the first hitting time of a Brownian motion (e.g.,
portnoy1978probability
;
lai1979first
).

Beyond asymptotics, non-asymptotic results are also essential, particularly when dealing with triangular arrays. For instance, motivated by applications to directed polymers,
busani2020bounds
studied the running maximum of partial sums with a small negative drift that scales with the sample size. Another example arises in survival bandit problems
veroutis2024survivalmultiarmedbanditsbootstrapping
, where agents aim to maximize rewards while minimizing the probability of ruin, i.e., the exhaustion of a budget.
The KMT inequality has not proven as powerful in that regime, notably because it depends on unknown constants, as noted in
busani2020bounds
. In this subsection, we demonstrate that
(\mathcal{D}_{k}(\alpha))
in
theorem

2.4
, which depends only on known constants, enables us to obtain computable non-asymptotic upper bounds for first hitting time probabilities.

To be more precise, in this subsection,
(X_{i}^{N})
denotes a triangular array of i.i.d. random variables with mean
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{N}]=\mu_{N}
and bounded support
X_{1}^{N}\in[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]
. We define
W_{i}^{N}:=\sum_{j\leq i}X_{j}^{N}
, and set

\tau_{N}:=\inf\{i\penalty\ \textrm{s.t.}\penalty\ W_{i}^{N}\geq g_{i}^{N}\},

(8)

where
(g_{i}^{N})
is a pre-specified boundary. For example,
busani2020bounds
considers the case where
\mu_{N}=\mu\sqrt{N}^{-1/2}
for some
\mu<0
and
g_{i}^{N}=x
. Our goal is to bound
\mathbb{P}\big(\tau_{N}\geq N\big)
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Assume that
(X_{i}^{N})
is a triangular array of i.i.d random variables satisfying
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{N}]=\mu_{N}
, and that for all
N
, we have
X_{1}^{N}\in[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]
. Let
\tau_{N}
be the stopping time defined in
eq.

8
. Let
\alpha>0
, and let
(\mathcal{D}_{k}^{N}(\alpha))
be the output of
algorithm

2
with
n=N
,
R=R
,
\sigma_{N}=\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(X_{1}^{N})}
, and confidence
\alpha
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tau_{N}\geq N)\leq\alpha+\mathbb{P}\Big(\forall i\leq N,\penalty\ B_{i}\leq\sigma_{N}^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(g_{i}^{N}-i\mu_{N}+\mathcal{D}_{i}^{N}(\alpha)}}\right)\Big),

where
(B_{i})
is a Brownian motion. Moreover, suppose that
\sigma^{*}=\inf_{N}\sigma_{N}>0
, then there exists a constant
\kappa_{R}
that only depends on
R
such that for all
\alpha>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tau_{N}\geq N)\leq\alpha+\mathbb{P}\Big(\forall i\leq N,\penalty\ B_{i}\leq\sigma_{N}^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(g_{i}^{N}-i\mu_{N}+\log N\kappa_{R}(\log N-\log\alpha)}}\right)\Big).

(9)

The proof can be found in
appendix

G
.
We note that the proposed upper bound only depends on the distribution of
(X_{i}^{N})
through its range, drift, and variance. In particular, this means the exact form of the distribution of
(X_{i}^{N})
is not required to compute this upper bound. When the variance is unknown, the bound can be adapted using the empirical bounds derived in
section

3
, and when the drift is unknown, it can be handled using valid anytime confidence sets (e.g
waudby2024estimating
).

Remark 4.4
.

To further bound
eq.

9
, one needs to approximate the crossing probability of the Brownian motion
\mathbb{P}\Big(\forall i\leq N,\penalty\ B_{i}\leq\sigma_{N}^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(g_{i}^{N}-i\mu_{N}+\mathcal{D}_{i}^{N}(\alpha)}}\right)\Big)
, which can either be done numerically or analytically
burq2008simulation
;
di2005simulation
;
karatzas2012brownian
.

As an example, we contrast our bound with the one obtained by another method in
busani2020bounds
. In that work, they assume a negative drift satisfying
-D_{\mu}(\log N)^{-3}\leq\mu_{N}\leq 0
for some finite constant
D_{\mu}>0
, and the boundary is a constant
g_{i}^{N}=x>0
. They provide a computable upper bound for
\mathbb{P}(\tau_{N}\geq N)
: Theorem 2.2 of
busani2020bounds
states that, under general conditions, for sufficiently large
N
and
x\geq(\log N)^{2}
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\tau_{N}\geq N\Big)\leq Cx(\log N)(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mu_{N}}}\right|\vee N^{-1/2}),

where
C
is a
computable
constant that depends on
R
and
\sigma
. Notably, if
\sigma=1
, it is easy to show that
C>4e^{56}
, which means the upper bound is trivial for any reasonable sample size.
If we choose
\alpha=1/N
, we can recover a similar rate to that of
busani2020bounds
. However, the constants are significantly smaller.

To demonstrate this, we derive the smallest
N
for which the upper bound in
eq.

9
becomes non-trivial when
\mu_{N}=\mu\sqrt{N}^{-1/2}
for
\mu=-0.1,-0.25,-0.5
and
g_{i}^{N}=10,20
.

Figure 2:
Minimum value of
N
for which the upper bound in
eq.

9
becomes non-trivial.

5
A Wasserstein-
p
bound for sequences sampled without replacement

A main ingredient of
algorithm

4
is our computable upper bound,
\omega_{p}^{R}(n,\sigma)
, for the Wasserstein-
p
distance between a partial sum and a normal distribution. To achieve this, we first adapt Theorem 5 of
bonis2015rates
to create an exchangeable pair method for bounding this distance for averages of items sampled
without
replacement.

More precisely, let
\mathcal{A}:=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\}
be a finite alphabet, and let
(X^{A}_{i})_{i\leq k}
designate a sequence of observations randomly drawn without replacement from
\mathcal{A}
. We define the centered average
W_{k}^{A}:=\sum_{i\leq k}X^{A}_{i}-\frac{k}{n}\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}
. For a large sample size
k
and alphabet size
n
, we can expect
W_{k}^{A}
to be almost normally distributed, and this can be quantified using
lemma

5.1
. More precisely, define
(\tilde{W}_{k}^{A},W_{k}^{A})
to be the exchangeable pairs such that

\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}X^{A}_{(I,J)(i)}-\frac{k}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X^{A}_{(I,J)(i)},

where
I\sim\textnormal{Uniform}\{1,\dots,k\}
and
J\sim\textnormal{Uniform}\{k+1,\dots,n\}
. Using this we obtain the following upper-bound.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\tilde{\kappa}>0
and
\eta^{2}>0
. Let
H_{k}(\cdot)
denote the
k
-th Hermite polynomial. Let
\tilde{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, and shorthand
H_{k}:=H_{k}(\tilde{Z})
. Then
for all
p\geq 1
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{p}(W^{A}_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,\eta^{2}))

(10)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\tilde{\kappa}}\Big\|e^{-t}W^{A}_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\eta\tilde{Z}\Big\|_{p}dt+\int_{\tilde{\kappa}}^{\infty}e^{-t}\|S_{p}(t)\|_{p}dt,

where

\displaystyle S_{p}(t):=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\frac{W_{k}^{A}-\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}}{s}-W_{k}^{A}|W_{k}^{A}\big]

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{-2t}\|H_{1}\|_{p}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\Big(\frac{1}{2s}\eta^{-1}\mathbb{E}\big[(\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}-W^{A}_{k})^{2}|W^{A}_{k}\big]-1\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\frac{e^{-\ell t}\|H_{\ell-1}\|_{p}}{s\ell!(\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}})^{\ell-1}}\eta^{-\ell+1}\mathbb{E}\big[(\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}-W^{A}_{k})^{\ell}|W_{k}^{A}\big].

The proof can be found in
section

C.1
.

Note that
lemma

5.1
builds on Theorem 5 of
bonis2020stein
but uses a specific Stein exchangeable pair. The proposed upper-bound can be further simplified if
\eta^{2}\approx\mathrm{Var}(W_{k}^{A})
.

When
\mathcal{A}:=\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\}
,
W_{k}^{A}
has the same distribution as
W_{k}
conditioned on
\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})
; that is,
W_{k}^{A}\overset{d}{=}W_{k}|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})
. Hence
lemma

5.1
can be used to obtain an upper bound for the conditional Wasserstein distance between
W_{k}
and a normal random variable. This leads us to the following computable upper bound:

Theorem 5.2
.

Suppose that
(X_{i})
satisfy Assumption (
R,\sigma
). For any positive integers
1\leq k\leq n
, the following inequality holds:

\Big\|\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{k(n-k)}{n}\sigma^{2})|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\Big)\Big\|_{p}\leq\omega^{R}_{p}(n,k,\sigma),

(11)

where
\omega^{R}_{p}(n,k,\sigma)
is defined in
theorem

C.15
,
eq.

28
. Moreover, if
n
is even, we define
\omega^{R}_{p}(n,\sigma):=\omega^{R}_{p}(n,\frac{n}{2},\sigma)
and obtain

\Big\|\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{n/2},\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{4})|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\Big)\Big\|_{p}\leq\omega^{R}_{p}(n,\sigma).

(12)

The proof can be found in
section

C.2
, and the exact expression of
\omega_{p}^{R}(n,k,\sigma)
is given in
eq.

28
within
theorem

C.15
.

In the following corollary, we show that
\omega_{p}^{R}(n,k,\sigma)
grows linearly in
p
and is bounded as
n,k
increase, meaning that

\sup_{n,k}\omega_{p}^{R}(n,k,\sigma)<\infty.

This matches the results obtained for the Wasserstein distance in the i.i.d. setting
bonis2020stein
;
austern2023efficient
, where no additional assumptions are made about the distribution of the random variables
(Y_{i})
, and shows that the dependence introduced by sampling without replacement does not change the rate of convergence of
W_{k}
.

Corollary 5.2.1
.

Suppose that
(Y_{i})
satisfies Assumption (
R,\sigma
) and that
n
is even. Then there exists a constant
\tilde{K}_{R,\sigma}<\infty
defined in
eq.

30
that only depends on
R
and
\sigma
such that for all
p\geq 2
we
have

\Big\|\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{k}^{2})|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\Big)\Big\|_{p}\leq\omega_{p}^{R}(n,k,\sigma)\leq{\tilde{K}_{R,\sigma}p}.

(13)

Moreover, for all
0<\sigma_{1}\leq\sigma_{2}\leq\frac{R}{2}
, we have

\sup_{\sigma\in[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]}\tilde{K}_{R,\sigma}<\infty.

The proof can be found in
section

C.3
.

Interestingly, we note that the Wasserstein-
p
distance
\Big\|\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{k}^{2})|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\Big)\Big\|_{p}
is uniformly upper bounded for all random variables
(Y_{i})
that are bounded by
R
and have a variance
\sigma
bounded away from
0.
This fact is crucial in the proof of
lemma

2.6
.

6
Discussion

This paper establishes a new computable version of the Komlós-Major-Tusnády (KMT) inequality, providing explicit bounds that depend only on the range and standard deviation of the random variables. We also derive an empirical version for cases where the standard deviation is unknown. The derived computable bounds thus make the KMT inequality a practical tool for deriving explicit, finite-sample guarantees.

Our proof rests on two key components: a novel inductive construction and a computable conditional Wasserstein-
p
bound. The inductive construction is inspired by a recent line of research that uses Stein’s method to provide simpler alternatives to the notoriously complex original KMT proofs, particularly the work of
chatterjee2012new
;
Bhattacharjee16
. However, a significant difference is that
Bhattacharjee16
;
chatterjee2012new
rely on bounding the moment generating function of the approximation error
|Z_{n}-S_{n}|
, which is done by constructing a bounded Stein kernel for
S_{n}
. This technique has not been successfully generalized to arbitrary bounded random variables. Our approach, in contrast, relies on controlling the conditional Wasserstein-
p
distance (for all
p\geq 2
) between the sum of variables sampled without replacement and a Gaussian random variable. We derive these bounds using an adaptation of the Stein’s method of exchangeable pairs from
bonis2015rates
.

The main drawback of our bound is a suboptimal asymptotic rate. Although in practice tighter than other computable bounds for any reasonable sample size (see
appendix

J
), our bounds are asymptotically larger by a logarithmic factor. This suboptimality stems from our method for bounding the conditional Wasserstein-
p
distance. Our bounds for the Wasserstein-
p
distance grow linearly with
p
(see
corollary

5.2.1
); the rate could be improved to
\log(n)^{3/2}
if this growth were reduced to
\sqrt{p}
, as is the case when the partial sum admits a bounded Stein kernel
chatterjee2012new
. A further improvement to the optimal
\log(n)
rate is possible if an almost sure bound on the conditional Wasserstein-
p
distance could be established, rather than the
L_{p}
norm bound we currently obtain. We leave these potential refinements for future work.

Although this work is limited to univariate bounded random variables, our framework is extensible to several other settings. An extension to the multivariate and sub-Gaussian settings would require developing a corresponding conditional Wasserstein-
p
bound for multivariate and sub-gaussian random variables, which could build upon recent work
bonis2023improved
. Furthermore, our framework could be adapted for variables that only have a finite number of moments. For variables with
q+2
finite moments, where strong approximation rates of
O(n^{1/q})
are known
major1976approximation
;
komlos1975approximation
;
komlos1976approximation
, deriving computable bounds would require adapting our Wasserstein-
p
bounds for
p\leq q
. We leave these extensions for future investigation.

Appendix A
Notations

We recall some notations used throughout the paper. Let
(Y_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be a sequence of i.i.d. random variables with
\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}]<\infty
,
\mathrm{Var}(Y_{1})=\sigma^{2}
, and
0\leq Y_{i}\leq R
almost surely.
Define
X_{i}=Y_{i}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}]
for
i\geq 1
.
Then
(X_{i})_{i\geq 1}
is a sequence of i.i.d. random variables with
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]=0
and
\mathrm{Var}(X_{1})=\sigma^{2}
. Let
(Z_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be a sequence of i.i.d. standard normal random variables, independent of
(X_{i})_{i\geq 1}
.

In addition, we use the following notation:

•

Denote
\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})
to be the unordered set of
\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
.

•

Let
Z,Z^{\prime}
be two random variables on the same probability space as
(X_{i})
.
We define the conditional Wasserstein-
p
distance as

\mathcal{W}_{p}(Z,Z^{\prime}|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})):=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(Z,Z^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))}\int|x-y|^{p}\,d\gamma(x,y)}}\right)^{1/p},

where
\Gamma(Z,Z^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))
is the set of all couplings whose marginals are the conditional distributions of
Z,Z^{\prime}
given
\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})
.

•

For
n>0
and
0<k<n
, define

S_{n}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad W_{k}:=S_{k}-\frac{k}{n}S_{n}.

We adopt the standard convention that
S_{0}=0
and
W_{0}=0
.

•

Let
(U_{i})_{i\geq 1}
denote a sequence of i.i.d
\rm{unif}[0,1]
random variables, independent of
(Y_{i})_{i\geq 1}
.

•

For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
H_{\ell}(\cdot)
to be the one-dimensional
\ell
-th Hermite polynomial
H_{\ell}(x):=e^{x^{2}/2}\frac{\partial^{\ell}}{\partial x}e^{-x^{2}/2}
and shorthand
H_{\ell}:=H_{\ell}(Z)
where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

•

Define
R_{s},\tilde{R}_{s},\tilde{R}
as
R_{s}:=\frac{1}{2}(R+\sqrt{R^{2}-4\sigma^{2}})
,
\tilde{R}_{s}:=R_{s}/\sigma
and
\tilde{R}:=R/\sigma
.

•

For
p>2
, define
A_{p}:=2^{1/p}\sqrt{p/2+1}e^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p}}
and
A_{n,p}^{*}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{2}+1}}\right)n^{1/p-1/2}
.

•

For
\sigma>0
, denote
\Phi_{\sigma}
the CDF of
N(0,\sigma^{2})
.

Appendix B
Preliminary Lemmas

Lemma B.1
.

Assume that
X_{1}
satisfies Assumption (
R,\sigma
). The following inequality holds

|X_{1}|\leq R_{s}.

Proof.

We remark that the following holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}^{2}]=\sigma^{2}+\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}]^{2}

Moreover, we also note that as
Y_{1}
is almost surely positive, so it follows that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}^{2}]\leq R\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}].
Hence we have that

\sigma^{2}+\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}]^{2}\leq R\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}].

This directly implies a lower bound for
\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}]
which used on the definition of
X_{1}
directly gives us the desired result.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let
X
be a random variable with finite support and CDF
F(\cdot)
, and probability mass function
p(\cdot)
. Let
F(x^{-})
denote the left-hand limit of
F(x)
. Let
U\sim\mathrm{Uniform}[0,1]
be independent of
X
. Define

{\tilde{F}^{U}}(x)=F(x^{-})+U\cdot p(x),

(14)

Let
\sigma>0
,
then the following holds:

\sigma\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{F}^{U}}(X))\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}).

Proof.

Suppose that the finite support of
X
is
\Omega=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{N}\}
, where we take
x_{1}<\dots<x_{N}
. We denote
p_{i}:=\mathbb{P}(X=x_{i})
and remark that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}=1
. For any
t\in[0,1]
, let
x_{i}
be the largest element of
\Omega
such that
F_{X}(x_{i}^{-})\leq t
. Then, by law of total probability,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\tilde{F}_{X}}(X)\leq t)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X<x_{i})+\mathbb{P}(F_{X}(X^{-})+U\cdot p_{X}(X)\leq t|X=x_{i})\mathbb{P}(X=x_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}p_{k}+\mathbb{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{i-1}p_{k}+U\cdot p_{i}\leq t)\cdot p_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}p_{k}+\frac{t-\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}p_{k}}{p_{i}}\cdot p_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t

Hence
{\tilde{F}_{X}}(X)\sim\text{Uniform}(0,1)
.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\mu
and
\nu
be probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
, and let
X\sim\mu
. Then there exists a random variable
Y\sim\nu
, measurable with respect to
X
and an independent
U\sim\textnormal{Uniform}[0,1]
, such that for all
\alpha>0
and all
p\geq 2
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(|X-Y|\geq\frac{\mathcal{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)}{\alpha^{1/p}}\Big)\leq\alpha.

Proof.

Define
Y:=\tilde{F}^{U}(X)
, where
\tilde{F}^{U}(\cdot)
is defined in
eq.

14
. By
lemma

B.2
, we have
Y\sim\nu
. Moreover, by Theorem 2.18 of
[
52
]
, we know that

\|X-Y\|_{p}=\mathcal{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu).

Now by Markov’s inequality, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(|X-Y|\geq\frac{\mathcal{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)}{\alpha^{1/p}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha\|X-Y\|_{p}^{p}}{\mathcal{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)^{p}}=\alpha.

∎

Lemma B.4
.

Define
\tilde{Z}_{n/2}:=\frac{\sqrt{n}\sigma}{2}\Phi^{-1}\Big(\tilde{F}_{n/2}^{U_{n/2}}(W_{n/2}))\Big)
, where
\tilde{F}_{n/2}^{U_{n/2}}(\cdot)
is defined in
eq.

1
. Then, for all
p\geq 1
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\Big\|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}\Big\|_{p}=\Big\|\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{n/2},\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}n/4)|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))\Big\|_{p}.

Proof.

By
lemma

B.2
, conditionally on
\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})
, we have that
\tilde{Z}_{n/2}\sim\mathcal{N}\Big(0,\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{4}\Big)
. Moreover according to Theorem 2.18 of
[
52
]
we obtain that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}\Big|^{p}\Big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\Big]^{1/p}=\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{n/2},\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}n/4)|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})).

Hence, by the tower law, we obtain that

\displaystyle\Big\|W_{n/2}-\tilde{Z}_{n/2}\Big\|_{p}=\Big\|\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{n/2},\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}n/4)|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))\Big\|_{p}.

∎

Lemma B.5

(Rosenthal’s inequality with explicit constants.)
.

Let
(\tilde{X}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be a sequence of centered i.i.d. observations. If
\|\tilde{X}_{1}\|_{p}<\infty
for some
p\geq 2
, then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}\tilde{X}_{i}}}\right\|_{p}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{2}+1}}\right)n^{1/p-1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\tilde{X}_{1}}}\right\|_{p}+2^{1/p}\sqrt{p/2+1}e^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\tilde{X}_{1}}}\right\|_{2}.

Proof.

See Lemma 14 in
[
2
]
.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs of
section

5

C.1
Proof of
lemma

5.1

C.1.1
Score bound for the conditional Wasserstein distance

Recall that
\mathcal{A}:=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\}
is a finite alphabet, and
(X_{i}^{A})_{i\leq k}
denotes a sequence drawn uniformly without replacement from
\mathcal{A}
. We define the centered sum
W_{k}^{A}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}^{A}-\frac{k}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}
, and its exchangeable counterpart
\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}X^{A}_{(I,J)(i)}-\frac{k}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X^{A}_{(I,J)(i)}
, where
I\sim\textnormal{Uniform}\{1,\dots,k\}
and
J\sim\textnormal{Uniform}\{k+1,\dots,n\}
.

In this subsection, we will derive an upper bound for
\big\|\mathcal{W}_{p}(W^{A}_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,\eta^{2}))\big\|_{p}
in terms of a score function. In this goal, we first remark that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{p}(W^{A}_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,\eta^{2}))

\displaystyle\leq\eta\mathcal{W}_{p}(\eta^{-1}W^{A}_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,1)).

Let
\tilde{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
be a standard normal, independent of
W^{A}_{k}
. We define the process
(X_{t})
to interpolate between
\eta^{-1}W_{k}^{A}
and
\tilde{Z}
in the following way

X_{t}:=e^{-t}\eta^{-1}W^{A}_{k}+\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}\tilde{Z}.

Denote
f_{t}
the density of
X_{t}
, meaning that for all
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
, we have
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in A)=\int_{A}f_{t}(x)dx
. Define
\xi_{t}:=(\log h_{t})^{\prime}(F_{t}),

where
h_{t}(\cdot)=\frac{f_{t}(\cdot)}{\varphi(\cdot)}
represents the conditional density of
F_{t}
with respect to the standard Gaussian measure. Then we use Lemma 2 of
[
41
]
.

Lemma C.1

(Lemma 2 of
[
41
]
)
.

For any
p>2
, under the above notations, the following inequality holds:

\mathcal{W}_{p}(\eta^{-1}W^{A}_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,1))\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\xi_{t}\|_{p}dt.

Using Lemma 5 from
[
7
]
, we directly obtain the following bound

Lemma C.2

(Theorem 5 from
[
7
]
)
.

Let
t>0
. The following equality holds:

\xi_{t}\overset{a.s}{=}\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{-t}\eta^{-1}W^{A}_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big|X_{t}\Big].

Those combined directly imply that

\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{k}^{A},\mathcal{N}(0,\eta^{2}))\leq\eta\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big\|\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{-t}\eta^{-1}W^{A}_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big|X_{t}\Big]\Big\|_{p}dt.

C.1.2
Bounding the score bound using Stein’s exchangeable pair

In this subsection we will provide a further upper-bound by using Stein’s exchangeable pair method. Throughout this section we will write

s:=\frac{n}{k(n-k)}.

Moreover we define

\tilde{W}^{A}_{k}
as

\tilde{W}^{A}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}X^{A}_{(I,J)(i)}-\frac{k}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{(I,J)(i)},

where
I\sim\mathrm{Unif}\{1,\dots,k\}
and
J\sim\mathrm{Unif}\{k+1,\dots,n\}
. Note that
(W^{A}_{k},\tilde{W}^{A}_{k})
form an exchangeable pair as we have

(W^{A}_{k},\tilde{W}^{A}_{k})\overset{d}{=}(\tilde{W}^{A}_{k},W^{A}_{k}).

Recall that we denote by
H_{\ell}(\cdot)
the one-dimensional
\ell
-th Hermite polynomial.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\tilde{\kappa}>0
. Assume that the conditions of
lemma

5.1
hold and shorthand
H_{k}:=H_{k}(\tilde{Z})
. Then for all
p\geq 2
, we have:

\displaystyle\eta^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{p}(W^{A}_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,\eta^{2}))

(15)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\tilde{\kappa}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|e^{-t}\eta^{-1}W^{A}_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big|^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}dt

\displaystyle+\eta^{-1}\int_{\tilde{\kappa}}^{\infty}e^{-t}\Big\|\frac{1}{s}\mathbb{E}\big[W^{A}_{k}-\tilde{W}^{A}_{k}|W^{A}_{k}\big]-W^{A}_{k}\Big\|_{p}dt

\displaystyle+\int_{\tilde{\kappa}}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-2t}\|H_{1}\|_{p}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\Big\|\frac{1}{2s}\eta^{-2}\mathbb{E}\big[(\tilde{W}^{A}_{k}-W^{A}_{k})^{2}|W^{A}_{k}\big]-1\Big\|_{p}dt

\displaystyle+\sum_{l\geq 3}\int_{\tilde{\kappa}}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-lt}\|H_{l-1}\|_{p}}{sl!(\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}})^{l-1}}\eta^{-\ell}\Big\|\mathbb{E}\big[(\tilde{W}^{A}_{k}-W^{A}_{k})^{\ell}|W^{A}_{k}\big]\Big\|_{p}dt.

Proof.

We introduce
\tau_{t}
similarly as in
[
7
]
:

\tau_{t}:=\sum_{\ell>0}\frac{e^{-\ell t}}{s^{\ell}\ell!\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\eta^{-\ell}\mathbb{E}[(\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}-W_{k}^{A})^{\ell}|W_{k}^{A}]H_{\ell-1}(\tilde{Z}).

(16)

Adapting the proof of Lemma 8 from
[
7
]
, we have

\mathbb{E}[\tau_{t}|X_{t}]=0\penalty\ a.s.

This directly implies that

\displaystyle\xi_{t}=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{-t}\eta^{-1}W^{A}_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big|X_{t}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\tau_{t}+e^{-t}\eta^{-1}W^{A}_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big|X_{t}\Big].

Using
lemma

K.4
, we then obtain that

\|\xi_{t}\|_{p}\leq e^{-t}\mathbb{E}[S_{p}(t)^{\frac{p}{2}}]^{1/p},

where

\displaystyle S_{p}(t):=

\displaystyle\eta^{-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\frac{W^{A}_{k}-\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}}{s}-W^{A}_{k}|W_{k}^{A}\big]

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{-2t}\|H_{1}\|_{p}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\Big(\frac{1}{2s}\eta^{-2}\mathbb{E}\big[(\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}-W_{k}^{A})^{2}|W_{k}^{A}\big]-1\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\frac{e^{-\ell t}\|H_{\ell-1}\|_{p}}{s\ell!(\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}})^{\ell-1}}\eta^{-\ell}\mathbb{E}\big[(\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}-W_{k}^{A})^{\ell}|W_{k}^{A}\big]

and where for ease of notations we shorthanded
H_{k}:=H_{k}(\tilde{Z}).

Finally, by combining this with
lemma

C.1
, we directly obtain that for all
\tilde{\kappa}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{p}(\eta^{-1}W_{k}^{A},\mathcal{N}(0,1))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tilde{\kappa}}\|\xi_{t}\|_{p}dt+\int_{\tilde{\kappa}}^{\infty}\|\xi_{t}\|_{p}dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tilde{\kappa}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|e^{-t}\eta^{-1}W^{A}_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big|^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}dt

\displaystyle+\eta^{-1}\int_{\tilde{\kappa}}^{\infty}e^{-t}\Big\|\frac{1}{s}\mathbb{E}\big[W^{A}_{k}-\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}|W_{k}^{A}\big]-W^{A}_{k}\Big\|_{p}dt

\displaystyle+\int_{\tilde{\kappa}}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-2t}\|H_{1}\|_{p}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\Big\|\frac{1}{2s}\eta^{-2}\mathbb{E}\big[(\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}-W_{k}^{A})^{2}|W_{k}^{A}\big]-1\Big\|_{p}dt

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\int_{\tilde{\kappa}}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\ell t}\|H_{\ell-1}\|_{p}}{s\ell!(\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}})^{\ell-1}}\eta^{-\ell}\Big\|\mathbb{E}\big[(\tilde{W}_{k}^{A}-W_{k}^{A})^{\ell}|W_{k}^{A}\big]\Big\|_{p}dt.

Therefore
eq.

18
follows.
∎

C.2
Proof of
theorem

5.2

In this subsection we apply
lemma

C.4
, to bound the conditional Wasserstein-
p
distance between
W_{k}
and
\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{k(n-k)}{n}\sigma^{2})
.

C.2.1
Bounding the conditional Wasserstein-
p
distance

Throughout this section we will write

s:=\frac{n}{k(n-k)}.

Moreover we define

W_{k}^{\prime}
as

W_{k}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}X_{(I,J)(i)}-\frac{k}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{(I,J)(i)}.

where
I\sim\mathrm{Unif}\{1,\dots,k\}
and
J\sim\mathrm{Unif}\{k+1,\dots,n\}
. Note that conditionally on
\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})
,
(W_{k},W^{\prime}_{k})
form an exchangeable pair, as we have

(W_{k},W_{k}^{\prime},\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))\overset{d}{=}(W^{\prime}_{k},W_{k},\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})).

Recall that we denote by
H_{\ell}(\cdot)
the one-dimensional
\ell
-th Hermite polynomial.

Lemma C.4
.

Let
\kappa>0
. Let
\tilde{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
be an independent normal. Assume that the conditions of
theorem

5.2
hold and shorthand
H_{k}:=H_{k}(\tilde{Z})
. Then if we define
\sigma_{n,k}^{2}:=\sigma^{2}\frac{k(n-k)}{n}
for all
p\geq 2
we have:

\displaystyle\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}\Big\|\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{n,k}^{2})|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))\Big\|_{p}

(17)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|e^{-t}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}W_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big|^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}dt

\displaystyle+\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}\int_{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}^{\infty}e^{-t}\Big\|\frac{1}{s}\mathbb{E}\big[W_{k}-W_{k}^{\prime}|W_{k},\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\big]-W_{k}\Big\|_{p}dt

\displaystyle+\int_{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-2t}\|H_{1}\|_{p}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\Big\|\frac{1}{2s}\sigma_{n,k}^{-2}\mathbb{E}\big[(W^{\prime}_{k}-W_{k})^{2}|W_{k},\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\big]-1\Big\|_{p}dt

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\int_{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\ell t}\|H_{\ell-1}\|_{p}}{s\ell!(\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}})^{\ell-1}}\sigma_{n,k}^{-\ell}\Big\|\mathbb{E}\big[(W_{k}^{\prime}-W_{k})^{\ell}|W_{k},\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\big]\Big\|_{p}dt

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle(a_{0})+(a_{1})+(a_{2})+(a_{3}).

Proof.

According to
lemma

5.1
applied with the alphabet
\mathcal{A}:=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
and
\tilde{\kappa}:=-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{n,k}^{2})\big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n}))

(18)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|e^{-t}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}W_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big|^{p}\Big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}dt

\displaystyle+\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}\int_{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}^{\infty}e^{-t}\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{s^{p}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\big[W_{k}-W^{\prime}_{k}|W_{k}\big]-W_{k}\Big|^{p}\Big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\Big]^{1/p}dt

\displaystyle+\int_{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-2t}\|H_{1}\|_{p}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|\frac{1}{2s}\sigma_{n,k}^{-2}\mathbb{E}\big[(W^{\prime}_{k}-W_{k})^{2}|W_{k}\big]-1\Big|^{p}\Big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\Big]^{1/p}dt

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell\geq 3}\int_{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\ell t}\|H_{\ell-1}\|_{p}}{s\ell!(\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}})^{\ell-1}}\sigma_{n,k}^{-\ell}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|\mathbb{E}\big[(W^{\prime}_{k}-W_{k})^{\ell}|W_{k}\big]\Big|^{p}\Big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\Big]^{1/p}dt.

The desired result directly follows from Jensen inequality.
∎

In the following subsections, we present lemmas that establish bounds for each term in
lemma

C.4
.

C.2.2
Bounding
(a_{0})

Lemma C.5
.

Assume that the conditions of
lemma

C.4
hold. Then the following bound holds for all
p\geq 2
:

\displaystyle(a_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{W}_{p}(\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}W_{k},\mathcal{N}(0,1))(1-\sqrt{1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}}})+\sqrt{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\Gamma((p+1)/2)}{\Gamma(1/2)}}}\right)^{1/p}\arccos{(\sqrt{1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}}})}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}\mathcal{S}^{p}_{\sigma,R}(n,k)\times(1-\sqrt{1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}}})+\sqrt{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\Gamma((p+1)/2)}{\Gamma(1/2)}}}\right)^{1/p}\arccos{(\sqrt{1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}}})}.

where
\mathcal{S}^{p}_{\sigma,R}(n,k)
is defined in
lemma

I.1
.

We note that the Wasserstein distance in
lemma

C.5
is the traditional Wasserstein-p distance and that we derive a numerical upper bound
\mathcal{S}^{p}_{\sigma,R}(n,k)
for it in
lemma

I.1
. This is similar to the upper bound already derived in
[
2
]
.

Proof.

Note that by definition of the Wasserstein-
p
distance, we know that for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a normal random variable
Z_{W_{k}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{n,k}^{2})
such that

\displaystyle\Big\|W_{k}-Z_{W_{k}}\Big\|_{p}\leq\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{k},Z_{W_{k}})+\varepsilon.

(19)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|e^{-t}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}W_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\|e^{-t}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}W_{k}-e^{-t}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}Z_{W_{k}}+e^{-t}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}Z_{W_{k}}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-t}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}\Big\|W_{k}-Z_{W_{k}}\Big\|_{p}+\Big\|e^{-t}Z_{W_{k}}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{-t}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}(W_{p}(\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}W_{k},Z_{W_{k}})+\varepsilon)+\frac{e^{-t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\|\tilde{Z}\|_{p}.

where the last inequality follows from
eq.

19
combined with the fact that
Z_{W_{k}}
and
\tilde{Z}
are independent. As
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, it follows that

\displaystyle\Big\|e^{-t}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}W_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{-t}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}W_{p}(W_{k},Z_{W_{k}})+\frac{e^{-t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\|\tilde{Z}\|_{p}.

Therefore, we obtain that

\displaystyle(a_{0})=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|e^{-t}\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}W_{k}-\frac{e^{-2t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}\tilde{Z}\Big|^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{k},Z_{W_{k}})\int_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}e^{-t}dt+\|\tilde{Z}\|_{p}\int_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}\frac{e^{-t}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}}dt

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle(a_{0,1})+(a_{0,2}).

We have

(a_{0.1})=\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{p}(W_{k},Z_{W_{k}})(1-{\sqrt{1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}}}}),

and

(a_{0,2})=\|\tilde{Z}\|_{p}\Big(\frac{\pi}{2}-\arcsin{(e^{-\kappa R})}\Big)=\sqrt{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\Gamma((p+1)/2)}{\Gamma(1/2)}}}\right)^{1/p}\arccos{(\sqrt{1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}}})}.

Hence the result follows.
∎

C.2.3
Bounding
(a_{1})

Recall the notation
R_{s}:=\frac{1}{2}(R+\sqrt{R^{2}-4\sigma^{2}})
, and that in
lemma

B.1
, we proved
|X_{1}|\overset{a.s}{\leq}R_{s}
. We also define
\tilde{R}_{s}:=\sigma^{-1}R_{s}.

Lemma C.6
.

Assume that the conditions of
lemma

C.4
hold then we have

\frac{1}{s}\mathbb{E}[W^{\prime}_{k}-W_{k}|W_{k},\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})]=-W_{k}.

Proof.

As
I\sim\textnormal{Uniform}\{1,\dots,k\}
and
J\sim\textnormal{Uniform}\{k+1,\dots,n\}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[W^{\prime}_{k}-W_{k}|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:k}),\mathcal{U}(X_{k+1:n})}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k(n-k)}{n}\mathbb{E}\bigg[(\sum_{i\leq k}X_{(I,J)(i)}-\frac{k}{n}S_{n})-(\sum_{i\leq k}X_{i}-\frac{k}{n}S_{n})\bigg|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:k}),\mathcal{U}(X_{k+1:n})\bigg]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{k(n-k)}{n}\mathbb{E}\Big[X_{J}-X_{I}\Big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:k}),\mathcal{U}(X_{k+1:n})\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k(n-k)}{n}\Big(\frac{1}{n-k}\sum_{k+1\leq j\leq n}X_{j}-\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i\leq k}X_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k}{n}(S_{n}-\sum_{i\leq k}X_{i})-\frac{n-k}{n}\sum_{i\leq k}X_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-W_{k}.

where (a) holds because
X_{(I,J)(i)}=X_{i}
if
i\neq I,J
. The desired result is a direct consequence of the tower property.
∎

Lemma C.7
.

Assume that the conditions of
lemma

C.4
hold. Then the following identity holds for all
p\geq 2
:

\displaystyle(a_{1})=0.

Proof.

Recall that

(a_{1})=\sigma_{n,k}^{-1}\int_{-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-\frac{R^{2}}{\kappa\sigma_{n,k}^{2}})}^{\infty}e^{-t}\Big\|\frac{1}{s}\mathbb{E}\big[W_{k}-W_{k}^{\prime}|W_{k},\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\big]-W_{k}\Big\|_{p}dt.

Note that, by
lemma

C.6
, we have
\Big\|\frac{1}{s}\mathbb{E}\big[W_{k}-W_{k}^{\prime}|W_{k},\mathcal{U}(X_{1:n})\big]-W_{k}\Big\|_{p}=0
. The result hence follows.
∎

C.2.4
Bounding
(a_{2})

Lemma C.8
.

Assume that the assumptions of
lemma

C.4
hold. Then the following upper-bounds holds:

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{1}{2s}\sigma_{n,k}^{-2}\mathbb{E}\Big[(W^{\prime}_{k}-W_{k})^{2}\Big|W_{k}\Big]-1\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{k(n-k)}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\min\Big\{\sqrt{p-1}(\tilde{R}^{2}_{s}-1)^{1-1/p},A_{p}\sqrt{\tilde{R}^{2}_{s}-1}+A^{*}_{n,p}(\tilde{R}^{2}_{s}-1)^{1-1/p}\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\min\Big\{\sqrt{p-1}\tilde{R}_{s}^{1-2/p},A_{p}+\tilde{R}_{s}^{1-2/p}A^{*}_{k,p}\Big\}^{2}\Big).

Proof.

By definition of
W^{\prime}_{k}
, and recall that
s=\frac{n}{k(n-k)}
, we remark that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2s}\mathbb{E}\Big[(W^{\prime}_{k}-W_{k})^{2}\Big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:k}),\mathcal{U}(X_{k+1:n})\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k(n-k)}{2n}\mathbb{E}\Big[(X_{J}-X_{I})^{2}\Big|\mathcal{U}(X_{1:k}),\mathcal{U}(X_{k+1:n})\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k(n-k)}{2n}\Big(\frac{1}{k(n-k)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
k<j\leq n\end{subarray}}(X_{j}-X_{i})^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
k<j\leq n\end{subarray}}(X_{j}^{2}+X_{i}^{2}-2X_{j}X_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2n}\Big((n-k)\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}X_{i}^{2}+k\sum_{k<j\leq n}X_{j}^{2}-2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
k<j\leq n\end{subarray}}X_{j}X_{i}\Big).

Hence we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{1}{2n}\Big((n-k)\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}X_{i}^{2}+k\sum_{k\leq i\leq n}X_{i}^{2}-2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
k<j\leq n\end{subarray}}X_{j}X_{i}\Big)-\sigma_{n,k}^{2}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{1}{2n}\Big((n-k)\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}X_{i}^{2}+k\sum_{k\leq i\leq n}X_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{n,k}^{2}\Big\|_{p}+\frac{1}{n}\Big\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
k<j\leq n\end{subarray}}X_{j}X_{i}\Big\|_{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{1}{2n}\Big((n-k)\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}(X_{i}^{2}-\sigma^{2})+k\sum_{k<i\leq n}(X_{i}^{2}-\sigma^{2})\Big)\Big\|_{p}+\frac{1}{n}\Big\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
